Title: On MUON optimization: From non-convergence to an error analysis with Polar Express and the Newton-Schulz polynomial from implementations

# On MUON optimization: From non-convergence to an error analysis with Polar Express and the Newton-Schulz polynomial from implementations 

## Thang Do 1,2, Steffen Dereich 3, and Arnulf Jentzen 4,5 

> 1

## August 6, 2026 

Abstract 

Stochastic gradient descent (SGD) optimization methods are the standard instruments for the training of deep neural networks (DNNs). In many relevant artificial intelligence (AI) systems – such as popular large language models (LLMs) – not the standard SGD scheme is used as the optimization method but instead suitable accelerated variants of SGD are employed. One of the most popular methods of such accelerated SGD variants is the momentum orthogonalized by Newton-Schulz (MUON) optimizer proposed by Jordan et al. in 2024. The MUON optimizer exploits the special matrix structure of the weight parameters in the training of the DNNs and, in its original form, employs five Newton-Schultz (NS) matrix steps in each MUON iteration. In this work we propose and study a generalized variant of the MUON optimizer involving an arbitrary number of generalized NS steps with polynomials of possibly arbitrary high degree. The considered optimizer covers MUON with the original NS polynomial as well as MUON combined with the recently proposed Polar Express method as special cases. For a simple class of stochastic optimization problems (SOPs) we show for almost every mini-batch size that MUON fails to converge 

to the solution of the SOP as the number of gradient steps converges to infinity. We also establish an error analysis for MUON with the generalized NS steps that provides convergence rates in terms of the number of gradient steps and in terms of the size of the mini-batch. We illustrate our general error analysis for MUON in the case of several concrete examples including quadratic stochastic optimization problems (SOPs) as well as ℓ2-regularized logistic regression for binary classification.

Stochastic gradient descent (SGD) optimization methods are the standard tools to train deep neural networks (DNNs) [61]. In many relevant artificial intelligence (AI) systems – such as popular large language models (LLMs) – not the standard SGD scheme is used as the optimization method but instead suitable sophisticated, accelerated variants of SGD are employed such as the famous adaptive moment estimation (Adam) optimizer [41] and its variant with decoupled weight decay referred to as Adam with decoupled weight decay (AdamW) optimizer [47]. For about one decade Adam and AdamW, respectively, have been the method of choice among the sophisticated optimizers for training large AI systems. However, in 2024 another highly competitive optimization method, referred to as momentum or-thogonalized by Newton-Schulz (MUON) optimizer, has been proposed and significantly influenced the state-of-the-art in the training of AI systems [38]. In particular, according to the intelligence score [2] the most intelligent LLMs, in which the choice of the optimizer has been publicly disclosed, are currently all trained with MUON, variants of MUON, or the AdamW optimizer, whereby MUON and its variants are significantly overrepresented compared to AdamW [14, Figure 1]. 

## 1.1 Generalized Newton-Schulz (NS) iterations 

The MUON optimizer employs the Newton-Schulz (NS) method (cf., for instance, Appendix A in [4], (5.22) in [29], and (6) and (8) in [73]) with the polynomial proposed in [38]. In this work we study MUON combined with generalized NS iterations. We first formulate the generalized NS iterations that we consider in Definition 1.1 below and, thereafter, we employ Definition 1.1 in the formulation of our error analysis for MUON in Theorem 1.2 below. The natural numbers n, m ∈ N = {1, 2, 3, . . . }

in Definition 1.1 represent the dimensionalities of the matrices to which we apply the generalized NS iterations.

## 1.2 First main result: Error analysis for MUON with generalized NS itera-tions 

Using the NS method from Definition 1.1 above we now present in the following theorem, Theorem 1.2, an error analysis result for the MUON optimizer. Theorem 1.2 is an immediate consequence of the more general result in Theorem 3.17 in Subsection 3.5 below (applied with p ↶ 2, U ↶ {x ∈ R𝒹 : ∥x∥ ≤ inf {ρ ∈ R : P(∥X1,1∥ ≤ ρ) = 1 }} in the notation of Theorem 3.17), which is one of the main results of this work. Theorem 3.17 treats a more general class of MUON optimization methods and also allows the optimization process to start in a random variable instead of a deterministic vector ξ ∈ Rd.We emphasize that the class of MUON optimization methods with generalized NS iterations described through different choices of the function b : ( N0)2 → R in Theorem 1.2 is so general that it covers the original MUON method with the original MUON polynomial [38] as implemented in the Pytorch library [1] (see Lemma 5.1 in Subsection 5.1 below) and MUON combined with the Polar Express method [3] as implemented in the modded-nanoGPT code [37] (see Lemma 5.2 in Subsection 5.2 below) as special cases. We also note that the assumption in Theorem 1.2 that lim sup n→∞ (γn + ( γn)−2(γn − γn+1 )) = 0 is equivalent to the conditions that lim sup n→∞ γn = 0 and lim sup n→∞ (( γn)−2(γn − γn+1 )) = 0. The condition lim sup n→∞ (( γn)−2(γn − γn+1 )) = 0 ensures that the sequence of learning rates ( γn)n∈N does not converge to quickly to zero (cf., for example, [14, Lemma 2.13]). These assumptions cover, for instance, polynomially decaying learning rates of the form γn = cn −r , n ∈ N, for arbitrary c ∈ (0 , ∞), 

r ∈ (0 , 1) (cf., for example, [14, Lemma 2.14]) and they have also been frequently used in error analyses for Adam and other SGD optimization methods (cf., for instance, [58, Assumption 2.2] and [17, Theorem 1.1]). 

## 1.3 Second main result: Non-convergence of MUON 

It is well-known that under suitable assumptions (cf. Theorem 1.2 above) we have that the mean square error of the standard SGD method after n ∈ N gradient steps can be bounded from above by a constant multiplied by the learning rate γn at step n. In (1.5) in Theorem 1.2 we only bound the mean square error of MUON with generalized NS iterations from above by a constant C multiplied by the sum of the learning rate γn and the reciprocal M −1 of the mini-batch size M . In the next result, Theorem 1.3, we prove that the stronger bound Cγn from standard SGD can in general not be established in the case of MUON. Specifically, in Theorem 1.3 we reveal for a simple quadratic SOP that for almost every mini-batch size M ∈ N we have that MUON (with generalized NS iterations) does not converge to the solution of SOP.

Theorem 1.3 is a direct consequence of the more general result in Theorem 6.16 in Section 6 below, which is one of the main results of this work. Theorem 6.16 treats a more general class of MUON-type optimization methods and also allows the optimization process to start in a random variable instead of a deterministic vector ξ ∈ Rd. In Theorem 1.3 we intend to solve the optimization problem argmin θ∈R L(θ) (1.9) where L : R → R is the function which satisfies for all θ ∈ R that L(θ) = E[L(θ, X 1,1)] = E[|θ − X1,1|2](the objective function, the function we intend to minimize). We observe that the objective function 

L : R → R has a unique global mimimizer at θ = E[X1,1]. In (1.8) in Theorem 1.3 we then reveal for almost every mini-batch size M ∈ N that we have that the considered generalized MUON opimization process (Θ Mn )n∈N0 fails to converge to the solution of the SOP as the number of gradient steps n converges to infinity. However, from (1.5) in Theorem 1.2 we see that the mean square error of the generalized MUON method can still be made arbitrarily small if, both, the number of gradient steps n and the mini-batch size M are sufficiently large. Theorem 1.3 only demonstrates that an additional error, beside the standard error term C√γn, must be included in the error estimates for MUON. The error of standard SGD and momentum SGD can, however, be bounded above only by C√γn; cf., [23, Theorem ...], [25, Theorem 5.8], [33, Theorem 3.7], [36, Proposition 3.3], and the references therein. 

## 1.4 An example: MUON for quadratic stochastic optimization problems (SOPs) 

A key feature of Theorem 1.2 and the more general result in Theorem 3.17, respectively, is that they are not merely abstract convergence results for the MUON optimizer, but that they also applicable to several concretely specified examples of SOPs. For example, Theorem 1.2 applies to this class of quadratic SOPs argmin θ=( θ1,...,θ δ )∈A EP δi=1 ∥Mi(θi) − pi(X1,1)∥2 (1.10) where A = ×δi=1 Rdi×di , where M1 : Rd1×d1 → Rd1×d1 , M2 : Rd2×d2 → Rd2×d2 , . . . , Mδ : Rdδ ×dδ → Rdδ ×dδ

are invertible linear functions, where p1 : R𝒹 → Rd1×d1 , p2 : R𝒹 → Rd2×d2 , . . . , pδ : R𝒹 → Rdδ ×dδ are Lipschitz continuous functions, where Xn,m : Ω → R𝒹 , ( n, m ) ∈ N2, are bounded i.i.d. random variables, where (Ω , F, P) is a probability space, and where d1, d 2, . . . , d δ , d1, d2, . . . , dδ ∈ N, δ, 𝒹 ∈ N.We refer to Lemma 4.3 and Corollary 4.5 in Subsection 4.2 below for an error analysis for MUON applied to a more general class of example SOPs (including (1.10) as a special case). Corollary 4.5 is based on an application of our more general error analysis result for the MUON optimizer in Theorem 3.17. Theorem 3.17 is also applicable to several other concretely specified optimization problems – including 

ℓ2-regularized logistic regression for binary classification (Subsection 4.1) – and we refer to Section 4 below for applications of Theorem 3.17 to some example optimization problems. 51.5 Literature review 

There is nowadays an extensive literature on the theoretical analysis of SGD optimization meth-ods , including works on the classical standard SGD method (cf., for example, [7]), articles on accelerated SGD optimizers such as Nesterov [52] (cf., for instance, [68]) and momentum SGD [57] (cf., for example, [70]), papers on adaptive SGD methods such as root mean square propagation (RMSprop) [30] (cf., for instance, [72]) and stochastic sign descent type methods [5] (cf., for example, [6, 12, 39, 62, 67]), as well as also a large number of works on accelerated and adaptive optimizers, such as the famous Adam [41] and AdamW [47] optimizers (cf., for instance, [13, 14, 17, 19, 45, 59, 71] and the references therein). The MUON optimizer, which is based on momentum SGD and approximate matrix normalization (approximate projection to the class of semi-orthogonal matrices; cf. (3.144) in Theorem 3.10 below), can be regarded as an optimizer that belongs to this last class of accelerated (due to the involved momentum) and adaptive (due to the approximate matrix normalization) SGD optimization methods, but also appends additional features as it respects the matrix structures of the parameters to optimize. Since its introduction in December 2024 [38], there have already appeared a series of works that study MUON-type methods analytically; see, for example, [8, 9, 27, 46, 50, 51, 54, 63, 64, 65, 66]. Some of these works analyze MUON-type methods in non-convex settings. For example, [27], [63, Theorem 3.1, Theorem 3.2, and Proposition 3.3], [64, Theorem 3], [65, Theorem 4.1], and [66, Theorem 2] neither assume strong convexity nor convexity but impose suitable L-smoothness assumptions (global Lipschitz continuity (with respect to different topologies) of the gradient of the objective function) and bounded variance conditions (global boundedness of the variance of the unbiased estimator of the gradient of the objective function) to establish for every T ∈ N upper bounds for the first absolute moment of the gradient of the objective function composed with the considered MUON optimization process evaluated at an on {0, 1, 2, . . . , T } uniformly distributed random variable (ergodic first-moment gradient bounds). In [46, Theorem 2.1], [51, Theorem 3.1], [65, Theorem 4.1], [63, Theorem 3.1 and Theorem 3.2], and [64, Algorithm 2] the authors study the idealized MUON optimizer (see [38, Section ”The design of Muon”] and, for example, [34, Definition 6.12.2] and Theorem 3.10 below), which corresponds to the situation where the number of NS steps converges to infinity and where appropriate parameters a, b,and c in the NS method in Definition 1.1 are chosen [27, Definition 2], and the work [27] studies MUON with a finite number of Newton-Schulz steps with a polynomial that ensures convergence to the idealized MUON optimizer (cf. also [63, Proposition 3.3]) but not with the polynomial that has been originally proposed for MUON [38] and that is used in the Pytorch library [1]. The work [64, Theorem 3] establishes such ergodic first-moment gradient bounds for stochastic Frank-Wolfe (FW) methods [22] and shows that MUON can be regarded as an instance of the considered stochastic FW schemes [64, Theorem 1]. In the work [8, Section 4.2] (cf., for instance, also [54, Section 4.2.2]) convergence rates for certain idealized MUON-type methods are shown under the assumption that the popular Polyak–Lojasiewicz inequality holds and under the assumption that the momentum decay parameter is not kept constant but increases to one during the training. This adjustment to increase the momentum decay parameter to one during the training allows them to establish convergence of MUON as the number of MUON steps go to infinity [8, Section 4.2]. In implementations the momentum decay parameter is, however, typically kept constant at the value α = 0 .95 [1, 37]. We also note that the findings in [8, Section 4.2] do not contradict to the non-convergence result for MUON in Theorem 1.3 above as the momentum decay parameter α ∈ (0 , 1) is kept constant during the training in Theorem 1.3 above (and as Theorem 1.3 considers MUON with the NS steps instead of idealized MUON-type methods). We also emphasize the instructive work [66] which studies a generalized MUON method that only incorporates approximations of the polar factor but not necessarily the exact polar factor in the idealized MUON method. In [66] it is assumed that the approximation errors (with respect to the exact polar factor) can be bounded by real numbers δk ≥ 0, k ∈ N0; see [66, Assumption 1]. A crucial assumption in the error analysis of [66] is then to assume that sup k∈N0 δk < 1 is strictly smaller than 1; see [66, Theorem 2 and Theorem 4]. The work [66] does not verify this assumption in the case of concrete optimization methods (cf. [66, Appendix A]) and in some settings this assumption excludes the NS iterations with the polynomial originally introduced within the MUON optimizer [38] as implemented in the Pytorch 

library [1] and also excludes the Polar Express method [3] as implemented in the modded-nanoGPT 

code [37]; see Lemma 5.3 and Lemma 5.4 in Subsection 5.3 below. To the best of our knowledge, Theorem 1.2 above and its generalization in Theorem 3.17 below, respectively, are the first results in the scientific literature that provide an error analysis for MUON with an arbitrary finite number of NS steps with the NS polynomial that has been originally proposed for MUON [38] and that is used in the Pytorch library [1] (corresponding to the choice that for all i, j ∈ N0

6it holds that bi,j = 3 .4445 1{1,2,..., 5}×{ 0}(i, j ) − 4.7750 1{1,2,..., 5}×{ 1}(i, j ) + 2 .0315 1{1,2,..., 5}×{ 2}(i, j ) + 

1{(0 ,0) }(i, j ) + 10 −71{(0 ,1) }(i, j ) in Definition 1.1; see Lemma 5.1 in Subsection 5.1 below) and also for MUON combined with the Polar Express method [3] as implemented in the modded-nanoGPT code [37] (see Lemma 5.2 in Subsection 5.2 below). In Theorem 1.3 above and Theorem 6.16 below we also establish non-convergence results for MUON . There are also a series of of lower error bounds, counterexamples, as well as non-convergence and divergence results for gradient based optimization methods in the literature. In particular, we refer, for example, to [10, 20, 21, 35, 48] for non-convergence results for SGD optimization methods in the training of artificial neural networks (ANNs) (some of them, showing that the probability to converge to a global minimizer is strictly positive or even large, close to one), we refer, for instance, to [24, 43, 49, 69] for divergence results for gradient based optimization processes in the training of ANNs (which provide sufficient conditions that the norm of the ANN parameter vector diverges during the training to infinity) (cf., for example, also [56, Section 1.2]), we refer, for instance, to [14, 28, 59] for non-convergence results specifically for the Adam optimizer studying the internal error of Adam (showing in certain examples that Adam does not converge to the solution (the unique minimizer) of the SOP under consideration), we refer, for example, to [39, Section 3] for non-convergence results for stochastic sign descent type optimization methods, and we refer, for instance, to [15] for non-convergence results for Adam and other SGD optimization processes where the learning rates (the step sizes) do not converge to zero. We also refer to [26, 55] for certain non-convergence results for MUON type methods. The work [55, Section 3] shows non-convergence of MUON to global minimum points for certain specific deterministic optimization problems with a non-smooth objective function. The non-smoothness of the objective function in [55] is essential and can not be avoided as MUON with asymptotically vanishing learning rates does converge to the global minimum point for smooth strongly convex deterministic optimization problems; cf. Corollary 4.7 and Theorem 3.10 below. The work [26, Section 3] studies a certain deterministic optimization problem in which the dynamics of the idealized MUON optimizer with a fixed step size becomes confined to discrete lattices and fails to converge. The non-convergence of the learning rates to zero is essential and can not be avoided as MUON with asymptotically vanishing learning rates applied to smooth strongly convex deterministic optimization problems does converge; cf. Corollary 4.7 and Theorem 3.10 below. We also refer to [3, Figure 3] for a numerical study that indicates that the NS method with the polynomial as originally proposed in [38] and as typically used in MUON implementations fails to converge to the polar factor. To the best of our knowledge, Theorem 1.3 above and its generalization in Theorem 6.16 below, respectively, are the first non-convergence results for the MUON optimizer in the literature that show that MUON (with the NS polynomial as originally proposed in [38] and as typically used in implementations) does, in general, not converge to the solution of the SOP (the minimizer of the SOP) as the number of gradient steps n converges to infinity. A crucial assumption in Theorem 1.3 and Theorem 6.16 is that the expectation of the third central moment of the data does not vanish E[( X1,1 − E[X1,1]) 3]̸ = 0. We recall that if the optimization problem is deterministic (corresponding to the case where the data X1,1 is one-point distributed, in which we have that E[( X1,1 − E[X1,1]) 3] = E[( E[X1,1] − E[X1,1]) 3] = 0), then convergence of MUON does hold (see Corollary 4.7 below) and the non-convergence conclusion in (1.8) in Theorem 1.3 can not hold anymore. 

## 1.6 Organization of this article 

The remainder of this work is organized in the following way. In Section 2 we establish pathwise a priori bounds, which are also of independent interest, for a general class of optimization methods including MUON as a special case; see Theorem 2.5 and Corollary 2.6. In Section 3 we first establish in Theorem 3.15 an error bound for the MUON optimizer under the assumption that the MUON optimization process is almost surely bounded by a fixed constant and, thereafter, we combine Theorem 3.15 with the a priori bound in Corollary 2.6 of Section 2 to establish in Theorem 3.17 an error estimate for MUON without assuming that the MUON optimization process stays bounded. Theorem 1.2 above in this introduction section follows from an application of Theorem 3.17 (see Subsection 3.5 for details). Furthermore, in Subsection 3.3 we also develop in Theorem 3.10 an error analysis for the idealized MUON optimizer (see [38, Section ”The design of Muon”] and, for example, [34, Definition 6.12.2]), which corresponds to the situation where the number of NS steps converges to infinity and where appropriate 7parameters a, b, and c in the NS method in Definition 1.1 are chosen. In Section 4 we illustrate the conclusion of Theorem 3.17 in the situation of several example opti-mization problems, namely, logistic regression for binary classification, quadratic SOPs, and deterministic optimization problems. In Section 5 we show that the framework in Theorem 1.2 and Theorem 3.17 is general enough to cover MUON combined with five NS steps with the original MUON polynomial [38] (see Lemma 5.1 in Subsection 5.1 below) as well as MUON combined with the Polar Express method [3] (see Lemma 5.2 in Subsection 5.2 below). In Section 6 we show for MUON optimization methods applied to a simple class of one-dimensional quadratic SOPs that for almost every mini-batch size M ∈ N we have that the optimizer fails to converge to the solution of the SOP (the unique global minimizer of the SOP) as the number of gradient steps 

n goes to infinity; see Theorem 6.16. Theorem 1.3 above in this introductory section is an immediate consequence of Theorem 6.16. 

## 1.7 Use of large language models 

Lemma 3.2, Proposition 3.3, Corollary 3.5, Theorem 3.6, Lemma 3.11, Lemma 6.1, Proposition 6.2, Lemma 6.6, Lemma 6.8, Lemma 6.9, Lemma 6.10, and Proposition 6.11 in this work have been developed based on discussions with GPT 5.5 Pro or GPT 5.6 Pro . Specifically, the core ideas for the proofs of Lemma 3.2, Proposition 3.3, Corollary 3.5, Theorem 3.6, Lemma 3.11, Lemma 6.1, Proposition 6.2, Lemma 6.6, Lemma 6.8, and Proposition 6.11 and the core ideas for the statements of Lemma 3.2, Proposition 3.3, Corollary 3.5, Lemma 6.1, Lemma 6.6, Lemma 6.8, Lemma 6.9, and Lemma 6.10 are due to GPT 5.5 Pro or GPT 5.6 Pro . In addition, GPT 5.5 Pro and GPT 5.6 Pro have supported us in creating the literature review in Subsection 1.5 above and have helped us to reveal several inaccuracies and errors within this manuscript. The entire article (including all statements and all arguments in this article) has been carefully written by the authors and the authors take full responsibility for each of the sentences/statements made in the paper. 

# 2 A priori bounds for MUON and closely related optimization methods 

In this section we establish in Theorem 2.5 and Corollary 2.6 pathwise a priori bounds for a general class of optimization methods including MUON as a special case. In Section 3 we employ the a priori bound in Corollary 2.6 to develop our error analysis for the MUON optimizer (see Theorem 3.17 below).

## 2.3 Qualitative pathwise a priori bounds for MUON type optimization pro-cesses

# 3 Error analysis for MUON and closely related optimization methods 

In this section we establish in Theorem 3.15 in Subsection 3.4 below an error bound for the MUON optimizer under the assumption that the MUON optimization process is almost surely bounded by a fixed constant and, thereafter, we combine Theorem 3.15 with the a priori bound in Corollary 2.6 of Section 2 to establish in Theorem 3.17 in Subsection 3.5 below an error estimate for MUON without assuming that the MUON optimization process stays bounded. Theorem 1.2 above in this introduction section follows from an application of Theorem 3.17. Our proof of Theorem 3.15 is based on an application of an error analysis for a general class of MUON-type optimization methods (which includes MUON as a special case) that we establish in Theorem 3.6 in Subsection 3.2 below. In our proof of Theorem 3.6 we first derive in (3.116) a recursive real inequality for the weak optimization errors ( E[|L (Θ ξ,M n ) − L (ϑ)|p]) 1/p for n ∈ N and, thereafter, we employ the estimate from Proposition 3.3 in this subsection below for solutions of the derived recursive inequality in (3.116) (cf. (3.20) in Proposition 3.3 below). Our proof of Proposition 3.3 is based on the elementary estimates for the recursive inequalities in Lemma 3.1 and Lemma 3.2. Only for completeness we include here detailed proofs for Lemma 3.1, Lemma 3.2, and Proposition 3.3. 

## 3.1 Upper estimates for solutions of some recursive inequalities for the anal-ysis of MUON 

Lemma 3.1. Let c ∈ R, τ ∈ (0 , ∞), N ∈ N and let γ : N → (0 , ∞) and e : N0 → [0 , ∞) satisfy for all n ∈ N ∩ (N, ∞) that

## 3.2 Error estimates for MUON-type optimizers under boundedness assump-tions 

Lemma 3.4. Let (Ω , F, P) be a probability space, let (U, U) be a measurable space, let (A, ⟨· , ·⟩ ) be a Hilbert space, let M ∈ N, α ∈ (0 , 1) , c, K ∈ (0 , ∞), p ∈ [1 , ∞), let Xn,m : Ω → U , (n, m ) ∈ N2,be i.i.d. random variables, let G : A × U → A be measurable, assume for all θ, ϑ ∈ A , x ∈ U with 

max {∥ θ∥, ∥ϑ∥} < c that 

∥G(θ, x )∥ ≤ K and ∥G(θ, x ) − G(ϑ, x )∥ ≤ K∥ θ − ϑ∥, (3.44)

Pni=1 (αn−iγi) ≤ K γn, (3.45)

## 3.3 Error estimates for idealized MUON under boundedness assumptions 

In this subsection we establish in Theorem 3.10 below an error analysis for the idealized MUON op-timizer (cf. [38, Section ”The design of Muon”] and, for instance, [34, Definition 6.12.2]). Our proof of Theorem 3.10 is based on an application of Theorem 3.6 above and also employs the elementary and well-known facts on (orthogonal) matrices in Lemma 3.7, Lemma 3.8, and Lemma 3.9. Only for completeness we include here detailed proofs for Lemma 3.7, Lemma 3.8, and Lemma 3.9.

Lemma 3.11. Let d, d ∈ N and let O = {O ∈ Rd×d : (( OO ⊤ = Id) ∨ (O⊤O = Id)}. Then (i) there exists a measurable Φ : Rd×d → O such that for all θ ∈ Rd×d it holds that ∥Φ( θ) − θ∥ =inf O∈O ∥O − θ∥ and (ii) it holds for every full-rank matrix θ ∈ Rd×d that there exists a unique A ∈ O such that 

∥A − θ∥ = inf O∈O ∥O − θ∥.

Proof of Lemma 3.11. Throughout this proof let P : Rd×d → R satisfy for all θ ∈ Rd×d that 

P (θ) = inf O∈O ∥O − θ∥ (3.152) and let H ⊆ Rd×d × O satisfy

∥A∥2 = min {d, d} ≤ d. (3.154) Hence, we obtain that O is bounded. Combining this, the fact that O is closed, and the fact that 

O ⊆ Rd×d proves that O is compact. In the following we prove that for all θ ∈ Rd×d there exists A ∈ O 

such that 

∥θ − A∥ = P (θ) = inf O∈O ∥θ − O∥ . (3.155) We prove (3.155) by contradiction. In the following we assume that there exists θ ∈ Rd×d which satisfies for all A ∈ O that 

∥A − θ∥ > inf O∈O ∥O − θ∥. (3.156) Note that there exists An ∈ O , n ∈ N, which satisfy lim n→∞ ∥An − θ∥ = P (θ) = inf O∈O ∥O − θ∥. (3.157) Observe that the fact that O is compact demonstrates that there exist ( nk)k∈N ⊆ N and B ∈ O which satisfy lim sup k→∞ ∥Ank − B∥ = 0 . (3.158) Note that (3.156), (3.157), and (3.158) establish that

P (θ) ≤ ∥ θ − B∥ ≤ ∥ θ − ϑ∥ + ∥ϑ − B∥ = P (ϑ) + ∥θ − ϑ∥. (3.160) Next we combine (3.152) and (3.155) to obtain that for all θ, ϑ ∈ Rd×d there exist A, B ∈ O such that

|P (θ) − P (ϑ)| ≤ ∥ θ − ϑ∥. (3.162) Hence, we obtain that P is continuous. Combining this and (3.153) shows that H is closed. This proves that H is measurable. Definition 3.14 (MUON process) . Let (Ω , F, P) be a probability space, let M, δ ∈ N,

d1, d 2, . . . , d δ , d1, d2, . . . , dδ ∈ N, α, χ ∈ R, let b = ( bi,j )(i,j )∈(N0)2 : ( N0)2 → R be a function, let A = ×δi=1 Rdi×di , let (U, U) be a measurable space, let L = ( L(θ, x )) (θ,x )∈A× U : A × U → R be measurable, assume for all x ∈ U that (A ∋ θ 7 → L(θ, x ) ∈ R) ∈ C1(A, R), let Xn,m : Ω → U ,

(n, m ) ∈ N2, be random variables, and let γ : N → (0 , ∞) be a function. Then we say that Θ is the α-γ-χ-b-M -MUON process for L on (Ω , F, P) with respect to (U, U) with data (Xn,m )(n,m )∈N2

(we say that Θ is the α-γ-χ-b-M -MUON process for L) if and only if there exist K ∈ N0 and 

35 m = ( m1, . . . , mδ ) : N0 × Ω → A such that (i) it holds that Θ = (Θ 1, . . . , Θδ ) : N0 × Ω → A is a stochastic process, (ii) it holds that Θ0 and (Xn,m )(n,m )∈N2 are independent, (iii) it holds that γ is non-increasing with lim sup n→∞ (γn + ( γn)−2(γn − γn+1 )) = 0 ,(iv) it holds that #( b−1(R\{ 0})) < ∞,(v) it holds for all i ∈ N ∩ [0 , K ], x ∈ R that P∞ 

> j=0

Note that the assumption that L is measurable, the assumption that for all x ∈ U it holds that (A ∋ θ 7 → L(θ, x ) ∈ R) ∈ C1(A, R), and, for instance, [34, Lemma 7.2.10] imply that A × U ∋ (θ, x ) 7 →

Theorem 3.15 (MUON error analysis under the assumption that MUON stays bounded) .

Let (Ω , F, P) be a probability space, let δ ∈ N, d1, d 2, . . . , d δ , d1, d2, . . . , dδ ∈ N, α ∈ (0 , 1) , let 

b : ( N0)2 → R be a function, let (U, U) be a measurable space, let Xn,m : Ω → U , (n, m ) ∈ N2, be i.i.d. random variables, let A = ×δi=1 Rdi×di , let K, p, χ ∈ (0 , ∞), let L = ( L(θ, x )) (θ,x )∈A× U : A ×

# 4 MUON applied to concrete examples SOPs 

In this section we illustrate Theorem 3.15 and Theorem 3.17 by means of several concrete example SOPs. Specifically, in Subsection 4.1 we apply Theorem 3.17 from Section 3 above to the situation of ℓ2-regularized logistic regression for binary classification (cf., for instance, [53, Section 1]), in Subsection 4.2 we apply Theorem 3.17 to a class of quadratic SOPs, and in Subsection 4.3 we apply Theorem 3.15 from Section 3 to the situation of deterministic optimization problems. 

## 4.1 MUON for logistic regression for binary classification 

In this subsection we show in the elementary fact in Lemma 4.1 that a class of ℓ2-regularized logistic regression problems satisfies the assumptions of the SOP in Theorem 3.17 and in Corollary 4.2 we specialize Theorem 3.17 to the situation where the considered SOP belongs to this simple class of ℓ2-regularized logistic regression problems. ## 4.3 MUON for deterministic optimization problems 

In this subsection we apply Theorem 3.15 above to the special situation of deterministic optimization problems. In Corollary 4.6 we assume the boundedness of the considered MUON optimization process and in Corollary 4.7 we restrict ourselves to the situation where the gradient of the objective function is Lipschitz continuous but then we do not assume anymore that the MUON optimization process is bounded.

# 5 Approximative orthogonalization methods within MUON 

In this section we show that the framework in Theorem 1.2 and Theorem 3.17 is general enough to cover MUON with five NS steps with the original MUON polynomial [38] as implemented in the Pytorch 

library [1] (see Lemma 5.1 in Subsection 5.1 below) and to cover MUON combined with the Polar Express method [3] as implemented in the modded-nanoGPT code [37] (see Lemma 5.2 in Subsection 5.2 below). In addition, inspired by the smallness condition that for all k ∈ N0 it holds that δk ≤ δ < 1 in [66, Assumption 1, Theorem 2, and Theorem 4] we establish in Subsection 5.3, in the situation of simple example optimization problems, lower bounds for the size of the error of orthogonalization methods within MUON (see Lemma 5.3 and Lemma 5.4 below).

# 6 Non-convergence of MUON 

The main purpose of this section is to prove Theorem 6.16 in Subsection 6.5 below. Theorem 6.16 shows for MUON optimization methods applied to a simple one-dimensional quadratic SOP that for almost every mini-batch size M ∈ N we have that the optimizer fails to converge to the solution of the SOP (the unique global minimizer of the SOP) as the number of gradient steps n goes to infinity. Theorem 6.16 covers MUON with an arbitrary finite number of NS steps with the original MUON polynomial [38] as implemented in the Pytorch library [1] (see Lemma 5.1 in Subsection 5.1 above) and MUON combined with the Polar Express method [3] as implemented in the modded-nanoGPT code [37] (see Lemma 5.2 in Subsection 5.2 above) as special cases. Our overall strategy of the proof of Theorem 6.16 is inspired by the arguments in our proof of the non-convergence part in the Adam symmetry theorem in our preliminary work [14, Theorem 1.1]. Specifically, we first establish in Proposition 6.2 in Subsection 6.1 a special case of a certain kind of MUON limit theorem , which shows for a simple one-dimensional quadratic SOP that if MUON converges to a vector 

ϑ, then this vector ϑ must be a zero of the MUON vector field introduced in (6.7) in Proposition 6.2. The purpose of Subsection 6.2, Subsection 6.3, and Subsection 6.4 is then to prove Corollary 6.14 in Subsection 6.4, which shows for almost every mini-batch size M ∈ N that the solution of the SOP is not a zero of the MUON vector field . In our proof of Theorem 6.16 in Subsection 6.5 we then combine the 

MUON limit theorem from Proposition 6.2 with the fact that for almost every mini-batch size M the solution of the SOP is not a zero of the MUON vector field according to Corollary 6.14 to see that that for almost every mini-batch size we have that MUON can not converge to the solution of the SOP. 51 6.1 MUON-type limit theorem 

In the next auxiliary result, Lemma 6.1, we briefly recall a simple consequence of the Stolz–Ces` aro theorem on Ces` aro summation limits with weights. Only for the sake of completeness we include here a short self-contained proof for Lemma 6.1. We employ Lemma 6.1 in our proof of the limit theorem for MUON-type methods in Proposition 6.2 below.

[14, Theorem 3.2]) establishes a result, which we refer to as Adam limit theorem that shows in a very wide generality that if the Adam optimizer converges in probability to any random variable ξ : Ω → Rd, then this random variable ξ must almost surely be a zero of the 

Adam vector field introduced in [17, Definition 2.4]. This Adam limit theorem is then used in [14] to prove non-convergence for Adam in the Adam symmetry theorem in [14, Theorem 1.1]. Inspired by this Adam limit theorem and its use to derive non-convergence properties for Adam, we establish in the next result, Proposition 6.2, in a very special case such a limit theorem for MUON and related methods. The SGD optimization methods in Proposition 6.2 are a general class of MUON-type methods and cover the MUON optimizer as a special case with an arbitrary number of NS steps and the NS polynomial as originally proposed in [38] and as used in practice (see (6.4) below, Lemma 3.13 above, and Lemma 6.15 below). The optimization problem class in Proposition 6.2 is, however, very restrictive and only applies to a very specific one-dimensional quadratic example class of optimization problems.

In (6.84) in Corollary 6.7 below we establish sharp convergence rates for the expectations of signed absolute powers of certain stationary momentum random variables. In our proof of Corollary 6.7 we employ the elementary fact on weakly convergent probability measures in Lemma 6.6 below. Only for completeness we include here a detailed proof for Lemma 6.6.

In the next elementary and well-known result, Lemma 6.10, we present a characterization for square integrable random variables with non-vanishing variance in terms of their characteristic functions. Only for completeness we include here a detailed proof for Lemma 6.10. 

Lemma 6.10 (Characteristic functions of random variables with non-vanishing variance) . Let 

(Ω , F, P) be a probability space and let Y : Ω → R be a random variable with E[Y 2] < ∞. Then the following two statements are equivalent: (i) It holds that Var( Y ) > 0.(ii) There exists δ ∈ (0 , ∞) such that for all t ∈ (0 , δ ) it holds that 

|E[exp( tY i)] | ≤ 1 − δt 2 ≤ exp( −δt 2). (6.135)

M ∈ N it holds that |zM | ≤ cM −r (convergence of zM with convergence rate r to zero) if and only if the rate of convergence r ≤ 3/2 is smaller or equal than 3/2.

## 6.4 Non-vanishing evaluations of the MUON vector field for non-symmetric data 

In the next elementary result, Lemma 6.12, we establish in (6.187) polynomial representations without quadratic terms for the NS functions. Lemma 6.12 is used in our proof of Proposition 6.13 below, which shows in (6.197) in the case of centered data E[X1,1] = 0 that the MUON vector field does not vanish at 0.

## 6.5 Non-convergence of MUON 

In the next elementary result, Lemma 6.15, we show for every d, d ∈ N, ε ∈ (0 , ∞), n ∈ N0 and appropriate a, b, c ∈ R that the function Rd×d ∋ A 7 → NS nb (A) ∈ Rd×d (cf. Definition 1.1) is globally Lipschitz continuous. 

Lemma 6.15 (Lipschitz continuity for the NS functions) . Let d, d ∈ N, K ∈ N0, let b =(bi,j )(i,j )∈(N0)2 : ( N0)2 → R satisfy min {b0,0, b 0,1} > 0 and #( b−1(R\{ 0})) < ∞, and assume for all i ∈ N ∩ [0 , K ], x ∈ R that P∞

(cf. Definition 1.1).
